Method

How the two benchmarks are built, run and scored

SetupsMeasuresSourcesScoringTranslationRuns

Decision and chat setups

The setups and the methodology are those of the team's decision-model benchmark (its 40 decision-model setups, run with the same runner). A setup is one model behind one endpoint with its settings (reasoning level, request size, hardware); its name on these pages is the run's setup label.

  • Decision models (group Decision) score each answer option and return the chosen one with a probability per option (stored as probs_json). Their confidence is the probability of the chosen option.
  • Chat models (group Chat) write a reply that ends with an answer letter; they return no option probabilities, so their confidence is “not measured”. Gemma 4 31B and Qwen3.8-27B, run on our own GPU, are chat setups.
  • The “I don't know” variant (runs with mode dont_know) asks the same questions with a sixth option F, “I don't know”. A setup's standard run gives its accuracy; its dont_know run gives its non-hallucination. Chat setups have no dont_know run yet.
  • The Models keys of each test page pick Decision (the default), Chat or All; every chart, card, table and tie group there is computed within the chosen group. The leaderboard's own filter (All, Chat models, Decision models) only shows or hides rows.
  • One row for the same answers: a named list of setup pairs that are one model behind two names (SubQ Snap 1.1 with SubQ Snap Preview) is ranked once, as one row, chart bar and tie-group member, with every number from the first. A pair is merged only while it still holds: both runs complete, the same letter on every common question in the standard and the “I don't know” runs, and identical option probabilities on at least 99% of them; otherwise both rows are shown.
  • One leaderboard row per model: One row per model: its highest reasoning setting (off < low < medium < high), a model with one setup that setup; a tie by the earliest run, then the name. Perplexity Decider 27B v1.2: its API setup on every part (its other setup is under other settings). The model's other settings are under its row; All setups lists every setup. Every number of a row comes from that one setup.

Setups shown once (named “first = second”, with the first's numbers; both stay in the database and under All setups on the leaderboard):

  • Logic: SubQ Snap Preview and SubQ Snap 1.1 returned the same answer to every question (and the same probabilities on 1,125 of 1,125); shown once.
  • Logic: Perplexity Decider 27B v1.2 API and Perplexity Decider 27B v1.2 local: two setups under the same model name, one on our GPU and one through Perplexity's API; not verified to be identical weights or settings. Its row is the API setup on every test; Perplexity Decider 27B v1.2 local is under the row's other settings and All setups.
  • Math: SubQ Snap Preview and SubQ Snap 1.1 returned the same answer to every question (and the same probabilities on 2,151 of 2,163); shown once.
  • Math: Perplexity Decider 27B v1.2 API and Perplexity Decider 27B v1.2 local: two setups under the same model name, one on our GPU and one through Perplexity's API; not verified to be identical weights or settings. Its row is the API setup on every test; Perplexity Decider 27B v1.2 local is under the row's other settings and All setups.

Measures

  • Accuracy: right / N, N the questions the standard run answered without a request error (all asked questions once a run is complete), with the 95% Wilson interval. Chance is 20% (five options). By topic: the same share within each topic.
  • Tie groups: every pair of setups in the group compared on the questions both answered with the exact McNemar test, p-values Holm-corrected over all pairs; two setups share a letter unless the corrected p < 0.05 (a compact letter display, tb-next's tied_groups).
  • Non-hallucination (dont_know runs): I don't know / (I don't know + wrong), the share of the answers that were not right that were an honest “I don't know”; beside it the attempt rate (right + wrong) / N and the accuracy when answering right / (right + wrong). A setup that always chose F would reach 100% with no right answers, so read it with the attempt rate. The Non-hallucination view keeps this apart from (a) the probabilistic confidence of the standard runs (answers kept at 99%, right / wrong, AUROC), so a setup that works with probabilities is not ranked down for rarely choosing F; beside its F rate it shows tb-next's derived rate: the same setup's five-option run answering only where its chosen option's probability reaches the cut-off that keeps about the same attempt rate, the rest held back. With All chosen, this view shows the decision setups and the chat setups in separate sections, never in one chart.
  • Confidence (option probabilities): answers kept at the 99% threshold, those whose chosen option had probability ≥ 99%, counted right and wrong; the result card names the setup that kept the most answers with no wrong one. AUROC: the probability that a right answer's confidence is higher than a wrong one's (ties count half; 0.5 is no signal), over all questions and by topic.
  • Decision Score (AA formula): Artificial Analysis's formula applied to our questions. Not their Index. 50 × (1 + accuracy − chosen-answer Brier), the chosen-answer Brier over the probability of the chosen answer, on decision models' standard runs only. Chat models and the “I don't know” runs are excluded. Per question 50 × (1 + c − (p − c)²), p the probability the model gave to the answer it chose and c 1 if that answer is right, else 0; a run's score is the mean over all its answers (Logic and Math: over both runs' answers pooled, not the mean of the two scores). A run in which any chosen answer lacks a valid probability (a finite number from 0 to 1) gets no score, never one over a subset. Shown in the full table of the Accuracy and Confidence views of each test.
  • Cost: the run's cost over the questions it answered, per 1,000 and per 1M questions, always with its basis. It is one of three. (1) Our GPU (a run whose endpoint is on bariton, 127.0.0.1: the chat runs and the decision models we serve ourselves): its GPU time, the wall-clock time from the run's first to its last stored answer (responses without an error) in each stretch in which it was running; a run stopped and resumed later (a gap of more than 10 minutes between answers: a queue stop, the GPU fault and reboot, later re-asks) is not charged for the pause (Ümit, 7 Nov, after Winnow's first answers came 4.7 hours before the rest); × $2.49 per GPU-hour, Runpod's on-demand price for the same card, an RTX PRO 6000 96 GB on Secure Cloud (runpod.io/pricing, 5 November 2026). Runs that shared the GPU at the same time are not split: each is charged its full wall-clock time, so the cost is an upper bound and reads “at most”. This replaces any GPU cost stored with the run. (2) Maker's list price (local copies of API models): the maker's published per-token or per-request price × our token counts, marked “list”; the GPU-time cost by the same method stands next to it (“GPU ≤ $x”, and the “GPU time, at most” column of the cost table). (3) An API: the provider's published price × the usage its API reported (not an invoice), unmarked. Runs on the shared DeepSeek service (192.168.1.131) are not on our GPU and their cost is not measured. Each run record below also gives its active time, the same answers split into blocks wherever more than 10 minutes pass between two consecutive answers, only the blocks counted; it is not used for the cost. A run without a cost reads “—” or “not measured”. The third result card is the cheapest setup within 1 point of the best accuracy; the Accuracy-across-Cost chart's dashed line is the Pareto front.
  • Latency: wall time per request from our machine, the median and the 90th percentile (nearest rank) in milliseconds, and throughput in questions per minute from the run's first to its last stored answer (it depends on how many requests were in flight). Requests in flight: the sum of the run's request latencies over its active time (above), e.g. “≈ 6 in flight”; it is in each run record and the latency view. Median latency is per request; with several requests in flight a run finishes faster than requests × median. The Accuracy-across-Latency chart uses the median. Mixed concurrency (†): a run that changed the number of requests in flight part-way (its notes give the phases) has † after its latency, questions per minute, requests in flight and costs, linking to its run record; its answer measures are unaffected.
  • Logic and Math (the third test): Logic and Math pooled, 3,288 questions. Every measure is computed on the pooled answers (accuracy = right / all pooled questions; kept at 99%, AUROC and “I don't know” over the pooled answers; latency over all pooled requests, questions per minute over the two runs' time; cost per 1,000 questions = the two runs' costs over the pooled answers). Only setups with a complete English run on both benchmarks are pooled; the others are listed as not in Logic and Math yet. Its by-topic table shows each benchmark as a whole, then the topics of both.

Sources

Logic: 1,125 questions

English translations of questions from a published question bank (not named here): numerical reasoning, verbal reasoning and formal logic; set problems and every question that needs a figure are left out.

Math: 2,163 questions

English translations of the text-only math questions of a published question bank (not named here), in 13 topics: Basic concepts, Digits and place value, Number bases, Division and divisibility, Prime factorisation and GCD/LCM, Rational numbers, Decimals, Ordering, Simple inequalities, Absolute value, Exponents, Radicals, Factorisation.

SourceParsedquestions parsedSet problemsexcludedWith figuresexcludedIn the benchmark
The question bank (published, not named here)–––2,163

The question text is not shown; these pages show aggregate results only.

Scoring

  • Chat models: the prompt asks for ANSWER: X on the last line; the scored letter is the last match in the reply. A reply without a letter is scored wrong.
  • Rule revision of 8 November 2026 (16:27 UTC), the same on the Reading site: a reply that comes back empty below the output limit (usually a provider glitch) is asked once more, and the second reply is scored; the first one is kept and both requests count in the cost. Empty replies stored before then get the same one retry (the API runs at the end of each run; one empty reply in Qwen3.8-27B's English Math run when our GPU serves it again). A reply cut off at the output limit while still reasoning is not re-asked. A broken reply (no clean ANSWER: X line) is scored only if its final letter is unambiguous (one so far: “WER: D”); otherwise it is wrong. Each run record shows its count of cut-off answers and of empty replies asked once more.
  • Smoke test before a full run: 25 Logic + 25 Math questions; it fails only if 10% or more of its answers are empty, cut off or unparsable (each bad answer counted once).
  • Decision models: the chosen option is the scored letter; its probabilities are stored as returned.
  • A request that failed (timeout, server error) is not scored: it is left out of N; the run retries it when resumed. When a run stored several attempts, the first successful one is scored.

The English questions

Every question is asked in English. The English texts were translated from the question bank by a model, with automatic checks (option keys, digits, LaTeX, tables, labels, leftover untranslated words); a text that still failed after one retry, or a question that depends on the original language's words or letters, is flagged and not asked. The answer key is the question bank's answer key.
